=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array maximumHeight, where maximumHeight[i] denotes the maximum height the i^th tower can be assigned.
Your task is to assign a height to each tower so that:

The height of the i^th tower is a positive integer and does not exceed maximumHeight[i].
No two towers have the same height.

Return the maximum possible total sum of the tower heights. If it's not possible to assign heights, return -1.
 
Example 1:

Input: maximumHeight = [2,3,4,3]
Output: 10
Explanation:
We can assign heights in the following way: [1, 2, 4, 3].

Example 2:

Input: maximumHeight = [15,10]
Output: 25
Explanation:
We can assign heights in the following way: [15, 10].

Example 3:

Input: maximumHeight = [2,2,1]
Output: -1
Explanation:
It's impossible to assign positive heights to each index so that no two towers have the same height.

 
Constraints:

1 <= maximumHeight.length <= 10^5
1 <= maximumHeight[i] <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)
        total = 0
        prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1  # first assignment will be capped by maximumHeight[0]
        for h in maximumHeight:
            cur = min(h, prev - 1)
            if cur <= 0:
                return -1
            total += cur
            prev = cur
        return total
```

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)
        total = 0
        prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1
        for h in maximumHeight:
            cur = min(h, prev - 1)
            if cur <= 0:
                return -1
            total += cur
            prev = cur
        return total
```

Let's decide final code. But before final, let's consider if the final code should be "```python ... ```" exactly. Yes.

Let's ensure code indentation and syntax:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumTotalSum(self, maximumHeight: List[int]) -> int:
        maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)
        total = 0
        prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1

        for h in maximumHeight:
            cur = min(h, prev - 1)
            if cur <= 0:
                return -1
            total += cur
            prev = cur

        return total
```
No syntax errors. `prev` is int. If maximumHeight[0] + 1 maybe large. Good.

```
if not maximumHeight:
    return 0
```

If h4<=0, greedy says impossible. Is there any assignment possible? Suppose h4<=0 means h3=1 and c4>=1 (or h3=0? Actually h4<=0 if h3-1<=0 or c4<=0. c4>=1, so h3=1). So h3=1. Then h2=2 (since h3=min(c3,h2-1)=1; if c3=1, then c4<=1, h4=0; if h2-1=1, h2=2). Cases:

- h3=1 because h2=2 and c3>1. Then h2=2. h2=2 because c2=2 or h1=3. If c2=2, then c3<=2, c4<=2. Used h2=2,h3=1. Need h4 positive distinct <=c4<=2; only 1,2 used, impossible. If h1=3, then h1=3. to this query.

The query is a competitive programming problem. I need to provide final Python code only, enclosed in a code block. I must reason internally but final output only code. Need solve.

Let's thoroughly reason.

Problem: Given array maximumHeight. Assign positive integer heights, distinct, <= each cap. Maximize sum. Return -1 if impossible.

Approach: Sort descending. Greedily assign largest possible height not used. Maintain `prev` = last assigned height. For each cap h, choose `cur = min(h, prev - 1)`. If cur <= 0 impossible. Add cur. This works because sorted descending, assigning largest available to largest cap maximizes sum and preserves feasibility. Need ensure if cur <=0 no possible assignment. Let's test edge cases.

Example [2,3,4,3] sorted [4,3,3,2]: cur4,3,2,1 sum10.
[15,10]: 15,10 sum25.
[2,2,1]: sorted [2,2,1]: cur2,1,0 return -1.

Need code with `List` import. Use `maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)`. `prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1` so first cur = min(max0, max0) = max0. Then loop.

Potential issue: If sorted list nonempty. constraints n>=1. Good.

Greedy seems robust.

Let's think of formal proof: Sort capacities descending. In an optimal assignment, we can assume heights are also descending and paired with capacities descending (exchange argument). Then for the first capacity, choose the largest possible height, c1. For the next, the largest unused height not exceeding c2 is min(c2, h1-1). Choosing any smaller height cannot improve later because later capacities are <= c2, and leaving a larger height unused while using a smaller one is never beneficial. Thus greedy.

Implementation details:
- `maximumHeight.sort(reverse=True)`
- `total = 0`
- `prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1` or `float('inf')`
- Loop:
```
for h in maximumHeight:
    cur = min(h, prev - 1)
    if cur <= 0:
        return -1
    total += cur
    prev = cur
return total
```
Need if `prev` is `float('inf')`, `prev - 1` is inf, min(h, inf) = h. But after first, prev int. Using `prev = maximumHeight[0] + 1` avoids float. Since n>=1. Good.

Let's test with [1]: prev=2, cur=min(1,1)=1, total1.
[1,1]: sort [1,1], prev=2, cur1, cur=min(1,0)=0 return -1.
[2,1]: sort [2,1], prev=3, cur2, cur=min(1,1)=1 total3.
[2,2]: sort [2,2], prev=3, cur2, cur=min(2,1)=1 total3.
